\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}\frac{\kappa}{12}K_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\sigma\tau}\left({\boldsymbol{k}},\boldsymbol{p}_{1},\boldsymbol{p}_{2},\boldsymbol{p}_{3},\boldsymbol{p}_{4}\right)\hat{\delta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{3}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}_{4})\ .

(82)

This interaction does not vanish when
\boldsymbol{k}=0
, and thus it contributes to the dynamics of the total spin.
However, we shall be careful not to confuse the absence of conservation with the presence of dissipation.
If the spin was largely dissipated, it would become a non-hydrodynamic variable whose behaviour does not affect that of the order parameter.
As shown in
Cavagna et al. 2021b
, where the fixed network approximation of the incompressible theory developed here is analyzed, even though the spin may not be globally conserved due to the DYS interaction, it represents still an hydrodynamic slow mode.
This is because the DYS vertex does not involve the spin, and thus represents the effects of the slow-mode
{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
. Even if this effect remains finite as
\boldsymbol{k}\to 0
, it may only be responsible for slow variations of the total spin
\boldsymbol{s}
.
Hence, although the total spin is not conserved, it is not even dissipated and thus it undergoes a generalized precession caused by the DYS vertex
Cavagna et al. 2021b
.

I.3
The diagrammatic expansion

I.3.1
Dynamical RG in momentum shell: general procedure

The momentum shell RG scheme
Wilson 1971
;
Goldenfeld 1992
;
Parisi 1988
;
Wilson and Kogut 1974
;
Cardy 1996
provides an explicit method to calculate critical exponents. By integrating out the short-wavelength modes iteratively, the RG generates a flow in the parameter space that eventually converges towards a fixed point. The study of the linearized flow equations near an attractive fixed point allows computing explicitly the critical exponents of the theory. Universality, in this RG context, means that one single fixed point rules the long-wavelength behaviour of a large class of theories, each one identified by a different initial point in the parameter space. The RG flow is defined by a set of recursive relations, obtained by iterating a RG transformation of the effective action of the field theory. The RG transformation consists of two steps:
i)
the probability distribution of the fields is marginalized by integrating short-wavelength modes on the shell
b^{-1}\Lambda<k<\Lambda
, with
b>1
, hence effectively decreasing the cutoff in momentum space;
ii)
space and time are rescaled, so to formally restore the same cutoff as the original theory.
The action obtained after one RG transformation has different parameters and it describes the system when it is observed on a larger scale.
However, since the partition function remains the same up to a multiplicative constant, the physical observables are left unchanged.
The flow equations are obtained under the form of recursive relations, describing how the parameters at the iteration
(l+1)
can be obtained starting from those at step
l
.

In the gaussian theory, namely when all interaction terms vanish, the shell integration is harmless since modes at different wavelengths are independent
Goldenfeld 1992
, and thus the RG flow is trivial: essentially each parameter rescales according to naive dimensional analysis.
However, when non-gaussian interactions are present, the shell integration couples long and short wavelength modes, generating nontrivial corrections to the bare action.
These corrections can be computed within a perturbative expansion in powers of
\epsilon=d_{c}-d
, where
d_{c}
is the upper critical dimension, namely the dimension above which mean-field theory is exact, while
d
is the spatial dimension.
This expansion method to compute the critical exponents, known as
\epsilon
-expansion, was proposed by K.G. Wilson, and first explored in the seminal paper
“Critical exponents in 3.99 dimensions”

Wilson and Fisher 1972
, in honour of which we chose the title of the present work.

In order to perform the shell integration, it is convenient to split the fields in their Infra-Red (IR) and Ultra-Violet (UV) modes
Goldenfeld 1992
, and write the partition function as,

\mathcal{Z}=\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}\,e^{-\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}]}=\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}\,e^{-\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]}

(83)

where
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}
stands for all the fields of the theory, while superscripts
<
and
>
indicate whether the field has momenta lower (IR modes) or higher (UV modes) than
\Lambda/b
respectively.
The action
\mathcal{S}
can then be written in the following form,

\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]=\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]+\mathcal{S}_{0}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]-\mathcal{V}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]

(84)

where
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is the gaussian part of the action while
\mathcal{V}
represents all the interactions between UV and IR modes.
Integrating out short wavelength details, namely performing the integration over the UV modes
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}
with wavevector on the shell, leads to,

\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{>}\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}e^{-\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]-\Delta\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]}

(85)

where,

e^{-\Delta\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{>}}\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}e^{-\mathcal{S}_{0}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]}e^{\mathcal{V}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]}

(86)

is the gaussian average of
e^{\mathcal{V}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}]}
over the UV fields with on-shell momentum, and
\Delta\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]
represents the corrections to the bare action
\mathcal{S}
due to the shell integration.
By assuming the interaction couplings
small
– assumption that will be verified
a posteriori
at the stable fixed point– it is possible to expand
e^{\mathcal{V}}
in powers of the couplings.
The expansion of
\Delta\mathcal{S}
can be graphically represented as an expansion in Feynman diagrams, composed only by connected diagrams
Goldenfeld 1992
.
This shell integration is usually performed under a thin-shell approximation, namely taking
b\sim 1
: in this limit
\Delta\mathcal{S}
is proportional to the shell thickness
1-b^{-1}\simeq\ln b
.
The final result after the integration over the UV modes
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{>}
is an effective action with a new cutoff
\Lambda/b
and modified parameters, namely,

\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathcal{P}_{0}+\mathcal{P}_{0}\delta\mathcal{P}\ln b=\mathcal{P}_{0}\left(1+\delta\mathcal{P}\ln b\right)\simeq\mathcal{P}_{0}b^{\delta\mathcal{P}}

(87)

where we used the relation
b^{a}\simeq 1+a\ln b
when
b
is close to
1
. The aim of the perturbative theory is to calculate the corrections to the parameters, namely the values of
\delta\mathcal{P}
.

In order to recover a theory with the same cutoff
\Lambda
as the bare theory momenta must be rescaled, which in turns requires to rescale also frequency and fields
Widom 1965
;
Kadanoff 1966
;
Ferrell et al. 1967
;
Halperin and Hohenberg 1967
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}=

\displaystyle b^{-1}\boldsymbol{k}_{b}

\displaystyle\omega=

\displaystyle b^{-z}\omega_{b}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)=}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{-\chi_{\phi}}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})}

(88)

where
z
is the dynamical critical exponent and
{\chi_{\phi}}
is the scaling dimension of the field. Once this rescaling is done,
the action takes the same form as the bare one, but with new renormalized parameters and couplings, which will be denoted with a subscript
b
. These new values of the parameters, defined in order to absorb all the powers of
b
in front of them, can be expressed as functions of the bare parameters through the following relation,

\mathcal{P}_{b}=b^{\chi_{\mathcal{P}}}\mathcal{P}_{0}

(89)

where
\chi_{\mathcal{P}}
defines the total scaling dimension of
\mathcal{P}
, which takes into account both the naive physical dimension, coming from dimensional analysis, and the anomalous scaling dimension due to the RG coupling of IR and UV modes.
We may therefore write
\chi_{\mathcal{P}}
as,

\chi_{\mathcal{P}}=d_{\mathcal{P}}+\delta\mathcal{P}\ ,

(90)

where
d_{\mathcal{P}}
is the
naive physical dimension of
\mathcal{P}
in units of momentum
k
, while
\delta\mathcal{P}
is the anomalous scaling dimension defined in Eq (
87
).
Thanks to this transformation, the partition function can be written as,

\mathcal{Z}\propto\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}e^{-(\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}]+\Delta\mathcal{S}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^{<}])}\propto\int\mathcal{D}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}e^{-\mathcal{S}_{b}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}]}

(91)

where
\mathcal{S}_{b}[{\boldsymbol{\phi}}]
is the
renormalized action
, obtained after integration over a shell of thickness
\ln b
and after rescaling.

I.3.2
Self-energies and vertex corrections

Following the RG scheme introduced in the previous Section we now derive the recursive relations for the parameters and coupling constants. As a consequence of the shell integration, the bare gaussian action
\mathcal{S}_{0}
of (
60
) and (
61
) acquires some corrections, that it is customary to write in the following way
Hohenberg and Halperin 1977
,

\begin{split}\Delta\mathcal{S}_{0}&=\int\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})-\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\tilde{\Sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\hat{\psi}_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})+\\
&+\int\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\Pi_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})-\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\tilde{\Pi}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\hat{s}_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})\ ,\end{split}

(92)

where all momenta are integrated off-shell,
k<\Lambda/b
, while frequency integrals still run form
-\infty
to
\infty
. The new quantities

\Sigma
,
\tilde{\Sigma}
,
\Pi
and
\tilde{\Pi}
are the
self-energies
, which contribute to the perturbative corrections of the gaussian parameters of the original action.
On the other hand, the interacting part of the action (
62
) acquires the corrections,

\begin{split}\Delta\mathcal{S}_{I}&=\int\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi s}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})+\\
&+\int\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\gamma}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2)\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2)+\\
&+\int\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})+\\
&+\int\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\eta}^{\hat{s}s\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\eta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})+\\
&+\int\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})+\\
&+\int\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\sigma\tau}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})\psi_{\sigma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})\psi_{\tau}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})\ ,\end{split}

(93)

where the various
vertex-functions

V
s give perturbative corrections to the coupling constants of the non-linear interactions.
We notice that all the corrections are proportional to the volume of the momentum shell, which is given by
1-b^{-1}\simeq\ln b
.
In what follows, we will compute the self-energies and the vertex functions by using perturbation theory.
Evaluation of perturbative corrections is done using a Feynman diagram expansion and considering only the first order in
\epsilon
.
Since we are interested in the dynamic behaviour near criticality, the mass
m
is set to
0
in all diagrams contributing to self-energies and vertex functions
Goldenfeld 1992
, except for those giving corrections to
m
itself.

As mentioned earlier, the self-energies represent the perturbative corrections to the gaussian part of the effective action. We distinguish four different self energies, one for each combination of fields appearing in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
, namely
\hat{\psi}\psi
,
\hat{\psi}\hat{\psi}
,
\hat{s}s
and
\hat{s}\hat{s}
.

From a diagrammatic point of view, each self-energy is given by the sum of all amputated 1-particle irreducible diagrams with external fields
\hat{\psi}\psi
,
\hat{\psi}\hat{\psi}
,
\hat{s}s
and
\hat{s}\hat{s}
respectively. Graphically, they are represeded by the blobs in the following diagrammatic scheme

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

(94)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\hat{\psi}_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

(95)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

(96)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\hat{s}_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

(97)

To identify perturbative corrections of the gaussian parameters, we need to expand the self energies in
\omega
and
\boldsymbol{k}
and keep only the leading terms.
By comparing the corrections
\Delta\mathcal{S}_{0}
in Eq. (
92
). with the form of the bare action
\mathcal{S}_{0}
in Eq. (
60
)-(
61
), we can define the perturbative corrections to the gaussian parameters through the relations,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

\displaystyle=\left[-\text{i}\omega\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}+m_{0}\,\delta m+k^{2}\Gamma_{0}\,\delta\Gamma\right]\ln b\ P_{\alpha\beta}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})+\dots

(98)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Gamma}_{0}\,\delta\tilde{\Gamma}\ln b\ P_{\alpha\beta}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})+\dots

(99)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\lambda_{0}^{\perp}\,\delta\lambda^{\perp}\ln b\ \mathbb{P}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\lambda_{0}^{\parallel}\,\delta\lambda^{\parallel}\ln b\ (\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P}^{\perp})_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\boldsymbol{k})+\dots

(100)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\perp}\,\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}\ln b\ \mathbb{P}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})+\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\parallel}\,\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}\ln b\ (\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P}^{\perp})_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}(\boldsymbol{k})+\dots

(101)

where we denoted the bare parameters with the subscript
0
and where the ellipses stand for higher orders terms in
\omega
and
\boldsymbol{k}
, which are irrelevant in determining the critical behaviour at first order in
\epsilon
. The
\ln b
factors present in all terms reflect the fact that perturbative corrections are proportional to the volume of the momentum shell.
After the shell integration, the gaussian action takes the following form,

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda/b}&=\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}\frac{\text{d}^{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega}{2\pi}\left[-\text{i}\omega\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}\ln b\right)\hat{\psi}\psi+\Gamma_{0}(1+\delta\Gamma\ln b)k^{2}\hat{\psi}\psi+m_{0}(1+\delta m\ln b)\hat{\psi}\psi\right]-\\
-&\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}\frac{\text{d}^{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega}{2\pi}\,\tilde{\Gamma}_{0}\left(1+\delta\tilde{\Gamma}\ln b\right)\hat{\psi}\hat{\psi}+\\
+&\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}\frac{\text{d}^{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega}{2\pi}\left[-\text{i}\omega\hat{s}s+\eta_{0}\hat{s}s+\lambda^{\perp}_{0}(1+\delta\lambda^{\perp}\ln b)k^{2}\hat{s}s+\lambda^{\parallel}_{0}(1+\delta\lambda^{\parallel}\ln b)k^{2}\hat{s}s\right]-\\
-&\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}\frac{\text{d}^{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega}{2\pi}\left[\tilde{\eta}_{0}\hat{s}\hat{s}+\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}_{0}(1+\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}\ln b)k^{2}\hat{s}\hat{s}+\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}_{0}(1+\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}\ln b)k^{2}\hat{s}\hat{s}\right]\end{split}

(102)

where we omitted the tensorial structure of the action to facilitate the reading.

The self-energies thus give perturbative corrections to the gaussian parameters
\Gamma
,
m
,
\tilde{\Gamma}
,
\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}
and
\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}
. Moreover, the self-energy
\Sigma
has also a non-vanishing term linear in
\omega
, namely
\delta\Omega
, which gives a perturbative correction to
-\text{i}\omega\hat{\psi}\psi
; because this term is not multiplied by any parameter in the original bare action, the only way to reabsorb this correction will be through a modification of the scaling dimension of the fields
\hat{\psi}
and
\psi
.
On the other hand, analogous perturbative terms do not arise in the self-energy
\Pi
, because all one-loop diagrams are at least of order
\boldsymbol{k}
as a consequence of the particular properties of the vertices (
79
) and (
81
), which vanish at
\boldsymbol{k}=0
, so that the term
-\text{i}\omega\hat{s}s
acquires only
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent perturbative corrections that vanish when
\boldsymbol{k}=0
.
Another – rather crucial – consequence of the fact that all corrections to
\Pi
and
\tilde{\Pi}
are proportional to
\boldsymbol{k}
, is that there are no perturbative corrections to neither
\eta_{0}\hat{s}s
nor
\tilde{\eta}_{0}\hat{s}\hat{s}
, and hence no
\delta\eta
and
\delta\tilde{\eta}
corrections are present in Eq. (
100
) and (
101
). This is no coincidence, but it is a result deeply related to the presence of the underlaying (rotational) symmetry of the problem. Although terms explicitly violating the conservation of the spin
s
are present, namely the dissipative terms
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
, the symmetry is still at work and the non-linear interactions are not able to generate any perturbative corrections to the dissipative coefficients
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
.

The standard way to explicitly perform this shell integration, and to compute the corrections to the bare parameters of the model, is using perturbation theory. The corrections
\delta\mathcal{P}
are thus computed using a Feynman diagram expansion.
At order
\epsilon
, the non-vanishing diagrams contributing the self-energies are listed in Section
II.1
.

Once the shell integration is performed, we are left with an effective action with a cutoff of
\Lambda/b
, a coefficient different from
1
in front of the
-\text{i}\omega\hat{\psi}\psi
term and modified parameters, namely

\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathcal{P}_{0}(1+\delta\mathcal{P}\ln b)\simeq\mathcal{P}_{0}b^{\delta\mathcal{P}}

(103)

Following Section
I.3.1
, we perform the following rescalings

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{k}=}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{-1}\,\boldsymbol{k}_{b}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\omega=}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{-z}\omega_{b}}

(104)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)=

\displaystyle b^{-\chi_{\psi}}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\psi}}}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)=

\displaystyle b^{-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\psi}}}(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})

(105)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)=

\displaystyle b^{-\chi_{s}}\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)=

\displaystyle b^{-\chi_{\hat{s}}}\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})

(106)

After this rescaling, we end up with an action with the same cutoff
\Lambda
but with new renormalized parameters and couplings, which will be denoted with a subscript
b
.

Let us at first focus on what happens to the
-\text{i}\omega
terms under rescaling. Once the shell integration is performed, the rescaling procedure defined by Eq. (
104
)-(
106
) transforms these terms as follows

\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}d^{d}k\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega\left[-\text{i}\omega\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}\ln b\right)\hat{\psi}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\omega)\psi(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)\right]\quad\Rightarrow\\
\Rightarrow\quad b^{-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-\chi_{\psi}-d-2z+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}}\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}_{b}\right|<\Lambda}d^{d}k_{b}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{b}\left[-\text{i}\omega_{b}\hat{\psi}(-\boldsymbol{k}_{b},-\omega_{b})\psi(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})\right]

(107)

\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}\right|<\Lambda/b}d^{d}k\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega\left[-\text{i}\omega\hat{s}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\omega)s(\boldsymbol{k},\omega)\right]\quad\Rightarrow\quad b^{-\chi_{\hat{s}}-\chi_{s}-d-2z}\int\limits_{\left|\boldsymbol{k}_{b}\right|<\Lambda}d^{d}k_{b}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{b}\left[-\text{i}\omega_{b}\hat{s}(-\boldsymbol{k}_{b},-\omega_{b})s(\boldsymbol{k}_{b},\omega_{b})\right]

(108)

where we remind that the
-\text{i}\omega\hat{s}s
term has no perturbative corrections because of the rotational symmetry.
As previously mentioned, these terms lack of a coupling constant to redefine. Hence, the scaling dimensions of the fields must be properly chosen to restore the same structure of the bare action, namely

\displaystyle\chi_{\hat{\psi}}+\chi_{\psi}=-d-2z+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{\hat{s}}+\chi_{s}=-d-2z

(109)

With this choice, the renormalized theory will still have a coefficient equal to one in front of the time derivative of the equation of motion. This requirement allows to fix the scaling dimension of the response fields
\chi_{\hat{\psi}}
and
\chi_{\hat{s}}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\hat{\psi}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\psi}-d-2z+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{\hat{s}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{s}-d-2z\,\ .

(110)

Rescaling can be performed on all the other terms of the action in the same way as it was done in Eq. (
107
)-(
108
). In these cases however, the powers of
b
appearing after rescaling can be reabsorbed by defining new
renormalized
parameters of the theory.
These new values of the parameters can be expressed as functions of the bare parameters,
through the perturbative corrections
, the dynamic exponent
z
and the
scaling dimensions

\chi
of the fields.

Scaling dimensions
for time and fields are not known
a priori
, but can be determined by imposing additional conditions.
For what concernes the gaussian action, the renormalized parameters are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}b^{\chi_{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-\chi_{\psi}-d-2-z+\delta\Gamma

(111)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{b}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Gamma}_{0}b^{\chi_{\tilde{\Gamma}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=-2\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-d-z+\delta\tilde{\Gamma}

(112)

\displaystyle\lambda_{b}^{\perp/\parallel}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}^{\perp/\parallel}b^{\chi_{\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{s}}-\chi_{s}-d-2-z+\delta\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}

(113)

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{b}^{\perp/\parallel}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\perp/\parallel}b^{\chi_{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}}

\displaystyle=-2\chi_{\hat{s}}-d-2-z+\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}

(114)

\displaystyle\eta_{b}

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}b^{\chi_{\eta}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\eta}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{s}}-\chi_{s}-d-z

(115)

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{b}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\eta}_{0}b^{\chi_{\tilde{\eta}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\eta}}

\displaystyle=-2\chi_{\hat{s}}-d-z

(116)

\displaystyle m_{b}

\displaystyle=m_{0}b^{\chi_{m}}

\displaystyle\chi_{m}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-\chi_{\psi}-d-z+\delta m

(117)

By taking advantage of Eq. (
110
) it is furthermore possible to write all the scaling dimensions of the parameters in terms of the scaling dimensions of the frequency and physical fields only, namely of
z
,
\chi_{\psi}
and
\chi_{s}
:

\displaystyle\chi_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=z-2+\delta\Gamma-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=2\chi_{\psi}+d+3z+\delta\tilde{\Gamma}-2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

(118)

\displaystyle\chi_{\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}}

\displaystyle=z-2+\delta\lambda^{\perp/\parallel}

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}}

\displaystyle=2\chi_{s}+d+3z-2+\delta\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp/\parallel}

(119)

\displaystyle\chi_{\eta}

\displaystyle=z

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\eta}}

\displaystyle=2\chi_{s}+d+3z

(120)

\displaystyle\chi_{m}

\displaystyle=z+\delta m-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

(121)

Let us note here a crucial fact: since the dynamic critical exponent
z
is always positive, so is the scaling dimension
\chi_{\eta}
of the dissipation
\eta
. Due to this,
\eta
grows exponentially when the RG transformation is iterated over and over, eventually diverging. This makes
\eta
a relevant parameter, with a role similar to that of the mass
m
: while
m
is the relevant parameter that drives the system away from the critical manifold, similarly
\eta
drives the system away from the conservative plane (while remaining on the critical manifold). Hence, independentely of all the other parameters, we expect
\eta
to diverge under the RG flow whenever its bare value is non-zero. This will have important consequences that we will discuss later on.

Now that we have defined the perturbative corrections to the gaussian parameters, we shall switch our attention to the coupling constants of the non-linear interactions.
Perturbative corrections to the interactions are known as
vertex functions
.
These vertex functions can be diagrammatically expressed through the sum of all the amputated connected diagrams having as external fields the same fields of the vertex they are correcting.
The six vertex functions of our theory, one for each bare vertex, can be graphycally represented through the blobs in the following expressions

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi s}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2+\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})

(122)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\eta}^{\hat{s}s\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
s_{\gamma\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
\psi_{\eta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2+\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})

(123)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\beta}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})
\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\sigma\tau}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\quad}

\hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})
\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})
\psi_{\sigma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})
\psi_{\tau}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})

(124)

At one-loop, the non-vanishing diagrams contributing to the vertices are listed in Section
II.2
. Each vertex function contributes to the corrections of couplings and parameters of Eqs. (
76
)-(
82
) in the following way,

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi s}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})=g\,\delta g_{\psi}\,P_{\alpha\rho}^{\perp}\left(\boldsymbol{k}\right)\mathbb{I}_{\rho\beta\gamma\nu}

(125)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})=-v_{0}\frac{\text{i}\gamma_{v}}{2}\delta\gamma_{v}P_{\alpha\beta\nu}\left(\boldsymbol{k}\right)

(126)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{\psi}\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}})=-\frac{J}{3}\delta J\,Q_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}\left(\boldsymbol{k}\right)

(127)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})=g\,\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}\left[\delta g_{s}\,\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\,\delta_{\sigma\tau}+\Phi_{1}\,\delta g_{s1}\,k_{\sigma}q_{\tau}+\Phi_{2}\,\delta g_{s2}\,q_{\sigma}k_{\tau}\right]

(128)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\eta}^{\hat{s}s\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})=-v_{0}\frac{\text{i}\gamma_{s}}{2}\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}\left[\delta\gamma_{s}\,k_{\eta}\delta_{\sigma\tau}+2\mu_{1}\,\delta\gamma_{s1}\,k_{\tau}\delta_{\sigma\eta}+2\mu_{2}\,\delta\gamma_{s2}\,k_{\sigma}\delta_{\tau\eta}\right]

(129)

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\sigma\tau}^{\hat{s}\psi\psi\psi\psi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{h}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}})=\frac{\kappa}{12}\delta\kappa\,K_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu\sigma\tau}\left({\boldsymbol{k}},\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{h},\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{h}-\boldsymbol{p}\right)

(130)

Higher orders in
\omega
and
\boldsymbol{k}
turn out to be irrelevant in determining the critical behaviour at first order in
\epsilon
.
The perturbative corrections to the anomalous terms have been defined as,

\displaystyle\delta g_{s1}\equiv\delta\left(g\,\Phi_{1}\right)\quad,\quad\delta g_{s2}\equiv\delta\left(g\,\Phi_{2}\right)

(131)

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{s1}\equiv\delta\left(\gamma_{s}\,\mu_{1}\right)\quad,\quad\delta\gamma_{s2}\equiv\delta\left(\gamma_{s}\,\mu_{2}\right)

(132)

(because they represent corrections to the
products

g\,\Phi_{1,2}
and
\gamma_{s}\,\mu_{1,2}
, they were not simply called

\delta\Phi_{1,2}
and
\delta\mu_{1,2}
).

After rescaling momenta, frequency and fields, we obtain the following RG transformations,

\displaystyle{\gamma_{\psi}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\gamma_{\psi}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\gamma_{\psi}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma_{\psi}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-2\chi_{\psi}-2d-1-2z+\delta\gamma_{\psi}

(133)

\displaystyle{\gamma_{s}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\gamma_{s}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\gamma_{s}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma_{s}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{s}}-\chi_{s}-\chi_{\psi}-2d-1-2z+\delta\gamma_{s}

(134)

\displaystyle{\mu_{1}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\mu_{1}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\mu_{1}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\delta\gamma_{s1}-\delta\gamma_{s}

(135)

\displaystyle{\mu_{2}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\mu_{2}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\mu_{2}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta\gamma_{s2}-\delta\gamma_{s}

(136)

\displaystyle g_{b}^{\left(\psi\right)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}b^{\chi_{g^{\left(\psi\right)}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{g^{\left(\psi\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-\chi_{\psi}-\chi_{s}-2d-2z+\delta g_{\psi}

(137)

\displaystyle g_{b}^{\left(s\right)}

\displaystyle=g_{0}b^{\chi_{g^{\left(s\right)}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{g^{\left(s\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{s}}-2\chi_{\psi}-2d-2-2z+\delta g_{s}

(138)

\displaystyle{\Phi_{1}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\Phi_{1}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\Phi_{1}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\Phi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\delta g_{s1}-\delta g_{s}

(139)

\displaystyle{\Phi_{2}}_{b}

\displaystyle={\Phi_{2}}_{0}b^{\chi_{\Phi_{2}}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\Phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta g_{s2}-\delta g_{s}

(140)

\displaystyle J_{b}

\displaystyle=J_{0}b^{\chi_{J}}

\displaystyle\chi_{J}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{\psi}}-3\chi_{\psi}-3d-3z+\delta J

(141)

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}b^{\chi_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\hat{s}}-4\chi_{\psi}-4d-4z+\delta\kappa

(142)

By using Eq. (
110
) we can write all the scaling dimensions of the interaction couplings in terms of the scaling dimensions of the frequency and physical fields, namely of
z
,
\chi_{\psi}
and
\chi_{s}
:

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma_{\psi}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\psi}-d-1+\delta\gamma_{\psi}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma_{s}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\psi}-d-1+\delta\gamma_{s}

(143)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\delta\gamma_{s1}-\delta\gamma_{s}

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta\gamma_{s2}-\delta\gamma_{s}

(144)

\displaystyle\chi_{g^{\left(\psi\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{s}-d+\delta g_{\psi}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{g^{\left(s\right)}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{s}-2\chi_{\psi}-d-2+\delta g_{s}

(145)

\displaystyle\chi_{\Phi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\delta g_{s1}-\delta g_{s}

\displaystyle\chi_{\Phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\delta g_{s2}-\delta g_{s}

(146)

\displaystyle\chi_{J}

\displaystyle=-2\chi_{\psi}-2d-z+\delta J-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}

\displaystyle\chi_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\chi_{s}-4\chi_{\psi}-3d-2z+\delta\kappa

(147)

By looking at Eqs. (
137
) and (
138
) it may seem that we have
two
different mode-coupling constants; however, this is not the case.
We defined two different renormalized coupling constants
g^{\left(\psi\right)}
and
g^{\left(s\right)}
only because the perturbative corrections
\delta g_{\psi}
and
\delta g_{s}
arising in Eqs. (
125
) and (
128
) are completely independent from each other, but this does not mean that there are two different
physical
constants. In fact, there must be only one mode-coupling constant, because
g
arises in the derivation of the equations of motion as the consequence of a symmetry, encoded by the Poisson-bracket relation
\{s,\psi\}\propto g\psi
, stating that
\boldsymbol{s}
is the generator of the rotations of
{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
.
The mode-coupling terms in both the equations of motion derive from this one Poisson-bracket relation; therefore, the existence of two different couplings would mean losing the connection with the underlying symmetry and Poisson structure. As we mentioned before, the scaling behaviour of the physical fields
{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
and
\boldsymbol{s}
is arbitrary, which allows a certain freedom in determining the scaling dimensions
\chi_{\psi}
and
\chi_{s}
, which enter Eqs. (
137
) and (
138
).
Hence, it is possible to use this freedom to restore the identity of the two mode-coupling constants,

{g_{b}^{\left(\psi\right)}}={g_{b}^{\left(s\right)}}=g_{b}

(148)

by simply asking that
\chi_{s}
is chosen in such a way that,

\chi_{g^{\left(\psi\right)}}=\chi_{g^{\left(s\right)}}

(149)

Essentially, we are requiring the field
\boldsymbol{s}
to scale in such a way that the coupling regulating the symmetry has one unique scaling behaviour.

I.3.3
A primer of the diagrammatic expansion

The one-loop Feynman diagrams generated by the action’s expansion, and necessary to calculate all the corrections
\delta\mathcal{P}
to the parameters and coupling constants, are listed in Section
II
; not only they are many, but they are also quite complicated due to the tensorial structure of the theory. Hence, we will perform in this Section the explicit calculation of one diagram, hoping that this may help the interested reader in picking up the general technique. The diagram we calculate is a contribution to the self-energy
\Sigma
defined in (
94
),

{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{{}}}
}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 137.8 -1.97 L 59.06 -1.97}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(150)

To facilitate the reader to follow the calculation and understand it better, we shall set
\eta=\tilde{\eta}=0
in the following paragraphs. Generalization of the following calculation in the case with
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
is straightforward.

The integral expression corresponding to this diagram can written following the definitions of lines and vertices given in Sections
I.2.2
and
I.2.3
,

D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=g^{2}\int\frac{d^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{d\omega_{p}}{2\pi}\ P_{\alpha\theta}(\boldsymbol{k})\mathbb{I}_{\theta\gamma\rho\sigma}\ \mathbb{G}_{\gamma\nu}^{0,\psi}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}-\boldsymbol{p},\frac{\omega_{k}}{2}-\omega_{p}\right)\mathbb{C}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}^{0,s}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\boldsymbol{p},\frac{\omega_{k}}{2}+\omega_{p}\right)P_{\nu\eta}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}-\boldsymbol{p}\right)\mathbb{I}_{\eta\beta\mu\lambda}

(151)

While the momentum integral is restricted to the momentum shell,
\Lambda/b<p<\Lambda
, the frequency integral is extended over the whole spectrum
(-\infty,+\infty)
. Furthermore, we notice that the integrand (
151
) depends on the frequency
\omega_{p}
only through the correlation functions
\mathbb{G}^{0,\psi}
and
\mathbb{C}^{0,s}
defined in Section
I.2.2
; this is in general true for any Feynman diagram present in this work. All the correlation functions (
67
)-(
70
) have at most two poles in the complex plane, and it is always possible to compute the frequency integral using the residue theorem.
In the case of this diagram the integration over
\omega_{p}
yields,

\begin{split}D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=g^{2}\int\frac{d^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}P_{\alpha\theta}(\boldsymbol{k})\mathbb{I}_{\theta\gamma\rho\sigma}P_{\nu\eta}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\boldsymbol{p})\mathbb{I}_{\eta\beta\mu\lambda}\left[\frac{2\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}\ \delta_{\gamma\nu}\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\boldsymbol{p})}{\lambda^{\perp}[\Gamma(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\boldsymbol{p})^{2}+\lambda^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k}/2+\boldsymbol{p})^{2}-\text{i}\omega_{k}+m]}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{2\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}\ \delta_{\gamma\nu}(\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\boldsymbol{p})-\mathbb{I}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda})}{\lambda^{\parallel}[\Gamma(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\boldsymbol{p})^{2}+\lambda^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{k}/2+\boldsymbol{p})^{2}-\text{i}\omega_{k}+m]}\right]\end{split}

(152)

Since we are going to work at first order in
\epsilon=4-d
(one loop), we can drop the
m
dependence in the Feynman diagrams, because this would lead to higher order corrections. Hence we set
m=0
in the following. The integral over the modulus of the momentum becomes trivial if we assume that the RG transformation is infinitesimal, namely if
b\simeq 1
; this means that the thickness of the momentum shell is infinitesimal and it is possible to approximate the integral of a generic function
f(\boldsymbol{p})
as follows,

\int_{\Lambda/b}^{\Lambda}d^{d}p\ f(\boldsymbol{p})=\int f(p)d\Omega_{d}p^{d-1}dp=\ln b\ \Lambda^{d}\int f(p)\biggl|_{|p|=\Lambda}d\Omega_{d}

(153)

which corresponds to approximating the integral as the value of the function at
|p|=\Lambda
times the volume of the momentum shell
\Lambda^{d}(1-1/b)\simeq\ln b
. After this step, the integrand may still depend on the direction of the momentum
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}=\boldsymbol{p}/|p|
, which is a unit versor. Applying this procedure to equation (
152
) leads to,

\begin{split}D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=g^{2}\int\frac{d\Omega_{d}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Lambda^{d}\ln b\ P_{\alpha\theta}(\boldsymbol{k})\mathbb{I}_{\theta\gamma\rho\sigma}&P_{\nu\eta}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})\mathbb{I}_{\eta\beta\mu\lambda}\left[\frac{2\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}\ \delta_{\gamma\nu}\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})}{\lambda^{\perp}[\Gamma(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})^{2}+\lambda^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k}/2+\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})^{2}-\text{i}\omega_{k}]}\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{2\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}\ \delta_{\gamma\nu}(\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})-\mathbb{I}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda})}{\lambda^{\parallel}[\Gamma(\boldsymbol{k}/2-\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})^{2}+\lambda^{\parallel}(\boldsymbol{k}/2+\Lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})^{2}-\text{i}\omega_{k}]}\right]\end{split}

(154)

where the versor
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}
is integrated over the
d
-dimensional sphere
\Omega_{d}
. The main advantage of having an expression of this kind is that all the dependence on the cut-off
\Lambda
is made explicit.

In order to recognize within this diagram the corrections to the various parameters of the original action, one has to expand in small external momenta and frequency, as we did in (
98
). The order at which it is convenient to perform this expansion depends on the particular Feynman diagram we are considering and the specific parameters or coupling constants that it corrects. For example, the diagram we are studying here gives rise to perturbative corrections to the
\hat{\psi}\psi
term in the Gaussian action (
60
); hence we must expand it up to the second order in the external momentum
\boldsymbol{k}
and up to the first order in the external frequency
\omega_{k}
. In this example we will calculate explicitly only the contribution of order
\omega_{k}
of this diagram, even though the diagram gives also contributions of
\mathcal{O}(1)
and of
\mathcal{O}(k^{2})
. The diagram at order
\omega_{k}
is,

D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=\text{i}\omega_{k}g^{2}\Lambda^{d-4}\ln b\ \int\frac{d\Omega_{d}}{(2\pi)^{d}}P_{\alpha\theta}(\boldsymbol{k})\mathbb{I}_{\theta\gamma\rho\sigma}P_{\nu\eta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})\mathbb{I}_{\eta\beta\mu\lambda}\delta_{\gamma\nu}\left[2\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}\frac{\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})}{\lambda^{\perp}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\perp})^{2}}+2\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}\frac{\mathbb{P}^{\perp}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}})-\mathbb{I}_{\rho\sigma\mu\lambda}}{\lambda^{\parallel}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\parallel})^{2}}\right]

(155)

Expanding all the tensors in equation (
155
), using the definitions (
37
), (
43
) and (
44
), we obtain,

D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=\text{i}\omega_{k}g^{2}\Lambda^{d-4}\ln b\ P_{\alpha\theta}(\boldsymbol{k})\int\frac{d\Omega_{d}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}{\lambda^{\perp}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\perp})^{2}}(1-d)\hat{p}_{\beta}\hat{p}_{\theta}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}{\lambda^{\parallel}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\parallel})^{2}}(d-2)(\hat{p}_{\beta}\hat{p}_{\theta}-\delta_{\alpha\beta})\right]

(156)

We can now perform the integral over the
d
-dimensional sphere
d\Omega_{d}
, which can be done using the following two relations,

\left\langle\hat{p}_{\alpha}\hat{p}_{\beta}\right\rangle_{\hat{p}}=\frac{1}{d}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\ ,\qquad\quad\left\langle\hat{p}_{\alpha}\hat{p}_{\beta}\hat{p}_{\gamma}\hat{p}_{\nu}\right\rangle_{\hat{p}}=\frac{1}{d(d+2)}\left(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\gamma\nu}+\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\nu}+\delta_{\alpha\nu}\delta_{\beta\gamma}\right)\ ,

(157)

where the brackets indicate the average over the
d
-dimensional sphere,

\langle\cdot\rangle=\frac{1}{\Omega_{d}}\int\ \cdot\ d\Omega_{d}

(158)

While the average of an even number of versors
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}
is nonzero, the angular average of an odd number of momenta vanishes by symmetry. This leads to the following expression for the diagram (
150
),

D_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\omega_{k})=-\text{i}\omega_{k}g^{2}\ \Lambda^{d-4}\frac{d-1}{d}\left[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}{\lambda^{\perp}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\perp})^{2}}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}{\lambda^{\parallel}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\parallel})^{2}}(d-2)\right]\ \ln b\ P_{\alpha\beta}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})

(159)

Since we are performing an
\epsilon
-expansion around the upper critical dimension
d_{c}=4
, we can set
d=4
in all the Feynman diagrams when results at first order in
\epsilon
are concerned. In the end, by comparing this expression to the expansion of the self-energy in (
98
), we can finally read the correction to the scaling dimension of the fields coming from this one diagram, namely,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\Omega}=g^{2}\left[\frac{3}{4}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}{\lambda^{\perp}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\perp})^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}{\lambda^{\parallel}(\Gamma+\lambda^{\parallel})^{2}}\right]+\dots

(160)

where the dots indicates the corrections from
all
other diagrams contributing to the self-energy
\Sigma
, which are listed in Section
II
.

I.3.4
Generation of the anomalous terms

The equations of motion proposed initially in (
24
), (
25
), in which the anomalous terms proportional to
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
,
\Phi_{1}
or
\Phi_{2}
do not appear, have a (relatively) clear physical interpretation. However, as previously pointed out, in the absence of some additional terms, which we call
anomalous
, the equations of motion are not RG-invariant; basically, what happens is that the RG generates some terms that were not present in the original action. To see how this happens, we perform here a shell integration starting from a theory with bare coefficients
{\Phi_{1}}_{0}={\Phi_{2}}_{0}={\mu_{1}}_{0}={\mu_{2}}_{0}=0
, and we show that these anomalous terms are spontaneously generated by the renormalization group transformation. We also hope that this further direct analysis of some highly nontrivial diagrams may provide the reader with a more robust technique to perform the entire calculation.
Since the generation of these non-linear terms does not rely on the presence of
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
, we shall set them both to
0
. Once again, we hope this makes the calculation easier to follow.

*** Anomalous Mode-Coupling terms

Without anomalies, the non Gaussian action (
62
) has only one term proportional to
\hat{s}\psi\psi
, corresponding to the following vertex,

\hbox to118.67pt{\vbox to90.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 19.56073pt\lower-45.32167pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{}{}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(161)

which is the same mode coupling vertex as (
80
), but without the anomalous terms. To compute the perturbative corrections to this vertex we must consider all the diagrams with an incoming
\hat{s}
line and two outcoming
\psi
lines; these are
12
diagrams, listed in Fig.(
14
) of Section
II
, most of which produce anomalous corrections. Here, as an example, we limit ourselves to consider only the first
two
of all these diagrams namely,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\ ,

(162)

where we explicitly write also the momenta of internal field lines. These diagrams can be converted into integral expressions following the usual Feynman rules. It is important to note, though, that this is exactly one of the cases in which it is more convenient to write the spin mode-coupling vertex as in (
79
) (but of course with
\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}=0
), rather than as in (
80
). The result is,

\begin{split}D^{\rm{mc},1}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}=&g^{3}\int\limits_{\Lambda/b<|\boldsymbol{p}|<\Lambda}\frac{\text{d}^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{p}+\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\boldsymbol{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]\left[\left(\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\boldsymbol{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]\times\\
&\times\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\sigma\rho}P_{\mu,\eta}^{\perp}\left(\boldsymbol{p}+\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)\mathbb{I}_{\eta\gamma\xi\zeta}\mathbb{I}_{\phi\chi\tau\nu}\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega_{p}}{2\pi}\mathbb{G}_{\rho\mu}^{0,\psi}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\mathbb{G}_{\xi\zeta\phi\chi}^{0,s}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}-\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\mathbb{C}_{\sigma\tau}^{0,\psi}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}-\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\ ,\end{split}

(163)

\begin{split}D^{\rm{mc},2}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}=&g^{3}\int\limits_{\Lambda/b<|\boldsymbol{p}|<\Lambda}\frac{\text{d}^{d}p}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{p}+\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\boldsymbol{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\sigma\rho}P_{\mu,\eta}^{\perp}\left(\boldsymbol{p}+\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)\mathbb{I}_{\eta\gamma\xi\zeta}P_{\sigma,\delta}^{\perp}\left(\boldsymbol{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{2}+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}}{2}\right)\mathbb{I}_{\phi\chi\tau\nu}\times\\
&\times\int\limits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\text{d}\omega_{p}}{2\pi}\mathbb{G}_{\rho\mu}^{0,\psi}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\mathbb{C}_{\xi\zeta\phi\chi}^{0,s}\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}-\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\mathbb{G}_{\delta\tau}^{0,\psi}\left(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{p}}+\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}}{2}-\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}}{2}\right)\ .\end{split}

(164)

Their sum is given by,

D^{\rm{mc},1}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}+D^{\rm{mc},2}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}=g\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}[\delta g_{s}\ \boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\delta_{\sigma\tau}\ +A\ k_{\sigma}q_{\tau}+B\ q_{\sigma}k_{\tau}]\ln b\quad\mbox{,}

(165)

where the constants
\delta g_{s}
,
A
and
B
are,

\begin{split}&\delta g_{s}=g^{2}w\frac{{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}w(w+1)^{2}+\tilde{\Gamma}x(w(x-5)-3x-1)-{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}(w-3)wx(w+x)}{12\Gamma^{3}(w+1)^{2}x(w+x)}\\
&A=-g^{2}w\frac{-3{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}w(w+1)^{2}+\tilde{\Gamma}x(w(6w+3x+11)+5x+3)-{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}w(3w+5)x(w+x)}{12\Gamma^{3}(w+1)^{2}x(w+x)}\\
&B=g^{2}w\frac{-5{\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp}}w(w+1)^{2}+\tilde{\Gamma}x(w(7-5x)-3x+5)+{\tilde{\lambda}^{\parallel}}w(5w+3)x(w+x)}{12\Gamma^{3}(w+1)^{2}x(w+x)}\end{split}

(166)

with
w=\Gamma/\lambda^{\parallel}
and
x=\lambda^{\perp}/\lambda^{\parallel}
. These two Feynman diagrams correct the interacting part of the action relative to the mode-coupling of the spin as follows,

\Delta\mathcal{S}_{\rm{mc}}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda/b}d\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}\ d\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}\ g\ \mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}\ \hat{s}_{\alpha\beta}(-\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}})[\underbrace{\delta g_{s}\ \boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\ \delta_{\sigma\tau}}_{\rm{bare\ structure}}+\underbrace{A\ k_{\sigma}q_{\tau}+B\ q_{\sigma}k_{\tau}}_{\rm{absent\ in\ the\ bare\ theory}}]\psi_{\gamma}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\psi_{\nu}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{k}}/2+\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\ln b\quad\mbox{,}

(167)

Note that, in terms of the corrections defined in Eq. (
128
), the terms
A
and
B
contribute to
\Phi_{1}\delta g_{s1}=A+\dots
and
\Phi_{2}\delta g_{s2}=B+\dots
, where the ellipses stand for contributions coming from diagrams other than
D^{\rm{mc},1}
and
D^{\rm{mc},2}
.

The core idea of the renormalization group is that the perturbative contributions generated by Feynman diagrams can be reabsorbed into a redefinition of the model’s parameters. Comparing equations (
161
) and (
167
) it is evident that the first term, proportional to
\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\nu}\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}
, has the same form as the bare vertex, hence it can be reabsorbed in the coupling,
g\to g(1+\delta g_{s}\ln b)
. However, we cannot reabsorb the second and third terms of equation (
167
) as correction of any pre-existing parameters, because of the different tensorial structure.
For this reason two novel terms, proportional to
\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}\ k_{\sigma}q_{\tau}
and
\mathbb{I}_{\alpha\beta\rho\sigma}\mathbb{I}_{\rho\tau\gamma\nu}\ q_{\sigma}k_{\tau}
respectively, must be included in the action.
These two terms coincide with the two
anomalous
terms
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
in equations (
62
).
We remind that most of the diagrams listed in Fig.(
14
) of Section
II

generate
the same anomalous terms with different coefficients
A
and
B
.
It is crucial to note that, if the model is at equilibrium,
v_{0}=0
,
\tilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma
and
\tilde{\lambda}^{\perp,\parallel}=\lambda^{\perp,\parallel}
, all these perturbative contributions vanish:
A=B=\delta g_{s}=0
, so that no anomalous terms are generated in the non-active case.

*** Anomalous Advection terms

The same procedure can be used to prove that the two anomalous advection terms, proportional to
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, are fundamental to guarantee the closure of the theory under the RG transformation. We use the same strategy: we assume that the anomalous terms are zero in the bare theory,
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
, then they are spontaneously generated by the renormalization group transformation. If we assume that
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=0
, there is only one term proportional to
\hat{s}s\psi
in the non Gaussian action (
62
), corresponding to the advective derivative. In the diagrammatic expansion, this corresponds to modifying the vertex,
